People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },

    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Well, I guess... I mean, work has been really intense lately. I'm a wholesale buyer, and honestly, sourcing decent materials right now is a total nightmare. Like, do you know how hard it is to get good cotton blends these days? My buddy Dave—he does logistics down in Miami—he was just telling me the shipping ports are completely backed up again. I actually bought a car from Miami a couple of years ago, a used Civic. Great gas mileage, but the AC died on me like two weeks after I drove it up here. Florida cars, man, you really have to watch out for the water damage. 

But anyway, yeah, what really made me come in today is... I just can't shake this feeling about my job. The people there, and even some of the external vendors we use, they're tracking my buys. I know they are. They keep messing with the launch numbers just to make me look stupid in front of everyone. And I've brought it up, but my supervisor just pulls up these reports and tries to show me that the data doesn't prove anything, like I'm just making it up. Which is ridiculous. She uses this weird Excel template for those reports, too, with these awful neon green headers. Like, who uses neon green for a financial spreadsheet? It literally hurts your eyes to look at. I told her she should switch to a soft blue, it’s way more professional and calming, you know? But yeah... they're definitely altering my numbers behind my back.

Yeah, it's... it's a mix, I guess. I get these stretches where I feel absolutely incredible, like the whole city is just opening up for me, you know? Like I'm completely dialed in. I asked for an iced Americano with a splash of oat milk, and she gave me hot drip coffee. And I just lost it on her. Like, I yelled at her in front of the whole store. No, it's not just when I'm wired like that. Even when I have a calmer stretch, you know? Florida cars, man, you really have to watch out for the water damage. \n\nBut anyway, yeah, what really made me come in today is... I just can't shake this feeling about my job. The people there, and even some of the external vendors we use, they're tracking my buys. I know they are. They keep messing with the launch numbers just to make me look stupid in front of everyone. And I've brought it up, but my supervisor just pulls up these reports and tries to show me that the data doesn't prove anything, like I'm just making it up. Which is ridiculous. She uses this weird Excel template for those reports, too, with these awful neon green headers. Like, who uses neon green for a financial spreadsheet? It literally hurts your eyes to look at. I told her she should switch to a soft blue, it’s way more professional and calming, you know? But yeah... they're definitely altering my numbers behind my back.\n\nDoctor: How certain are you that people at work are altering your numbers behind your back?\n\nPatient: I'm totally certain. Like, a hundred percent. I just can't shake the feeling, you know? It's like this gut instinct. Kind of like when I was at the store on Tuesday, I just knew they were going to be out of the mango energy drinks I always get. And they were! The guy stocking the shelves—he was wearing this really weird vintage fedora, which, I mean, who wears a fedora to stock shelves? It looked ridiculous. He said they haven't had a shipment in weeks. \n\nBut anyway, yeah, with work... even when my supervisor shows me those reports and says the data doesn't prove anything, I just know they're tracking my buys and messing with the launch numbers. They're doing it on purpose just to make me look stupid in front of everybody. The vendors are definitely in on it too. Speaking of vendors, one of them sent us this massive fruit basket for the holidays last year, and I swear, half the pears were completely bruised. Like, if you're going to send a corporate gift to your buyers, at least make sure the fruit is actually fresh, right? But yeah, I'm absolutely sure about the numbers.\n\nDoctor: Have you recently had a period where you felt unusually energetic and needed much less sleep than usual?\n\nPatient: Yeah, definitely. There have been plenty of weeks where I sleep maybe three or four hours, max. And I wake up feeling totally wired, just ready to go. Lying in bed honestly feels like I'm just wasting time I could be using to make moves, you know? Like, why sleep when there's so much to get done? \n\nI actually used that extra time a couple weeks ago to completely reorganize my closet. I color-coded all my shirts, which, let me tell you, is a game changer. I bought these velvet hangers online—they were on sale, like fifty percent off, which is a steal because normally they're way too expensive for what they are. The velvet really keeps the shirts from slipping off. My old plastic hangers used to drop my button-downs all the time, especially the silk blend ones I wear when I have vendor meetings. I got this one blue silk shirt at a thrift store downtown, the one next to that amazing taco truck. Man, their al pastor tacos are incredible. The guy who runs it, Hector, he always gives me extra pineapple on top. Pineapple is really the secret to a good al pastor, it cuts through the grease perfectly. \n\nBut yeah, anyway, sleeping just feels pointless when I have that much energy. Three hours is plenty.\n\nDoctor: During those times when you sleep only a few hours, do you feel unusually euphoric or irritable?\n\nPatient: Yeah, it's... it's a mix, I guess. I get these stretches where I feel absolutely incredible, like the whole city is just opening up for me, you know? Like I'm completely dialed in. But then, if someone questions a purchase order or tells me I need to slow down, I snap. Like, way harder than I mean to. I just go off. \n\nIt's kind of like what happened at the coffee shop the other day. I was in line, feeling great, and the barista—she had this really elaborate sleeve tattoo, all these koi fish and lotus flowers, the line work was actually super impressive. I was thinking about getting a tattoo once, maybe a geometric wolf on my forearm, but I could never decide on the artist. There's a guy in Brooklyn who specializes in dot work, but his waitlist is like a year long. Anyway, the barista messed up my order. I asked for an iced Americano with a splash of oat milk, and she gave me hot drip coffee. And I just lost it on her. Like, I yelled at her in front of the whole store. I felt a little bad later, but in the moment, it just made me so incredibly mad. So yeah, I definitely get pretty irritable when I'm feeling that energetic.\n\nDoctor: Do you have this belief that people at work are tracking your buys and altering your numbers only when you're in those high-energy, low-sleep periods?\n\nPatient: No, it's not just when I'm wired like that. Even when I have a calmer stretch, you know? Like, there was this period where I was actually sleeping normally, maybe seven or eight hours, and I didn't feel like I was running the whole market or anything. But I still spent a few weeks totally knowing they were tracking my buys and messing with the numbers. And I was still hearing that voice in the stockroom. \n\nOur stockroom is actually a nightmare, by the way. The lighting in there is this awful fluorescent buzz that gives me a headache after five minutes. They keep saying they're going to upgrade to LEDs, but maintenance is dragging their feet. The maintenance guy, Gary, he's always taking these ridiculous long smoke breaks out by the loading dock. He smokes those little filtered cigars, the ones that smell like cheap vanilla. My grandpa used to smoke those when we'd go fishing up at the lake. We caught this massive bass once, I swear it was like ten pounds. We had it mounted and it hung in his garage for years until the garage roof leaked and ruined it. It's a shame, really. But yeah, even when I'm sleeping fine and feeling normal, I still know they're tracking me and I still hear that voice.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }
]

{"candidates":["F31.2","F20.0","F23","F22","F60.0"],"note":"Persistent persecutory delusions and auditory hallucinations outside mood episodes increase likelihood of a schizophrenia-spectrum disorder, especially F20.0; mood-congruent psychosis limited to mania is less likely, but bipolar with psychotic symptoms remains possible. Acute/transient psychosis is less favored if symptoms are persistent, and paranoid personality alone is less likely given fixed delusions/hallucinations.","is_final":false}

People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there.   },

    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah, it's definitely an actual voice. It's out loud. Sometimes when I'm in the stockroom, or even when I'm just driving back from a vendor meeting, I'll hear this man's voice. It sounds like it's coming from right behind me, just saying, "Move faster, Scott," or he'll just start laughing at the items I picked for the order. And every time, I spin around to look, and there's absolutely nobody there. 

It's really distracting when I'm driving, especially on the interstate. The traffic on I-95 has been completely insane lately because of that construction near the bridge. They've had the right lane closed for, like, six months now. I don't even know what they're fixing. I saw this huge crane there the other day, one of those massive yellow ones with the giant treads instead of wheels. I used to have a toy crane just like that when I was a kid. My dad bought it for me at this little hobby shop that used to be next to the bowling alley. We used to go bowling every Thursday night, and they had the best french fries. They were crinkle-cut and always super salty, and they served them in those little red plastic baskets with the wax paper liners. I tried to make crinkle-cut fries at home once, but I bought the wrong kind of potatoes. I used russets, but I think you're supposed to use Yukon Golds or something if you want them really crispy. 

But yeah, the voice is out loud. It's right there behind me, telling me to move faster or laughing at me.

The maintenance guy, Gary, he's always taking these ridiculous long smoke breaks out by the loading dock. He smokes those little filtered cigars, the ones that smell like cheap vanilla. My grandpa used to smoke those when we'd go fishing up at the lake. We caught this massive bass once, I swear it was like ten pounds. We had it mounted and it hung in his garage for years until the garage roof leaked and ruined it. It's a shame, really. But yeah, even when I'm sleeping fine and feeling normal, I still know they're tracking me and I still hear that voice.\n\nDoctor: When you hear that voice in the stockroom, does it sound like an actual voice speaking out loud?\n\nPatient: Yeah, it's definitely an actual voice. It's out loud. Sometimes when I'm in the stockroom, or even when I'm just driving back from a vendor meeting, I'll hear this man's voice. It sounds like it's coming from right behind me, just saying, \"Move faster, Scott,\" or he'll just start laughing at the items I picked for the order. And every time, I spin around to look, and there's absolutely nobody there. \n\nIt's really distracting when I'm driving, especially on the interstate. The traffic on I-95 has been completely insane lately because of that construction near the bridge. They've had the right lane closed for, like, six months now. I don't even know what they're fixing. I saw this huge crane there the other day, one of those massive yellow ones with the giant treads instead of wheels. I used to have a toy crane just like that when I was a kid. My dad bought it for me at this little hobby shop that used to be next to the bowling alley. We used to go bowling every Thursday night, and they had the best french fries. They were crinkle-cut and always super salty, and they served them in those little red plastic baskets with the wax paper liners. I tried to make crinkle-cut fries at home once, but I bought the wrong kind of potatoes. I used russets, but I think you're supposed to use Yukon Golds or something if you want them really crispy. \n\nBut yeah, the voice is out loud. It's right there behind me, telling me to move faster or laughing at me.\n\n[Last Question]\nDid the voice and the belief that people at work were tracking your buys start very suddenly, within a couple of weeks?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. If the question touches several topics, return the one it is\n  most directly about — never more than one.\n- Copy `key`, `name` and `description` exactly as they appear in [Symptom Profiles].\n- If your profile does not cover what the doctor asked, return the text \"None\" for `key`,\n  `name` and `description`, and say why in `reason`. Do NOT force an unrelated item.\n- On your first reply of the consultation, always return the item whose key is your\n  chief_complaint_symptom_code, whatever the doctor opened with.\n- Do not answer the doctor, and do not write anything outside the JSON.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"key\": \"<key>\", \"name\": \"<name>\", \"description\": \"<description>\", \"reason\": \"<why this item>\"}\n"
  },

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Um, no, I wouldn't say it was sudden. It just kind of... I don't know, it crept up on me, I guess. It wasn't like one day it was fine and the next I was hearing him. It was more gradual. Kind of like how the seasons change, you know? Like, you don't realize it's autumn until you step outside and suddenly the air just has that crisp smell to it. 

I love that smell, actually. It always reminds me of this apple orchard my mom used to take me to when I was little. We'd go every October, and they had these incredible cider donuts. They were always served warm, right out of the fryer, and just coated in cinnamon sugar. I've been trying to find a bakery around here that makes them like that, but nobody gets it right. They're always too dense or they use too much nutmeg. Nutmeg is such an overpowering spice, you really have to be careful with it. I put too much in a pumpkin pie I tried to bake for Thanksgiving two years ago, and it was practically inedible. My sister still teases me about it. She's actually coming to visit next month, and I need to figure out where we're going to eat. She's a vegetarian now, which makes picking a restaurant so complicated. 

But yeah, no, it wasn't a sudden thing. It just slowly started happening.

It just kind of... I don't know, it crept up on me, I guess. It wasn't like one day it was fine and the next I was hearing him. It was more gradual. Kind of like how the seasons change, you know? Like, you don't realize it's autumn until you step outside and suddenly the air just has that crisp smell to it. \n\nI love that smell, actually. It always reminds me of this apple orchard my mom used to take me to when I was little. We'd go every October, and they had these incredible cider donuts. They were always served warm, right out of the fryer, and just coated in cinnamon sugar. I've been trying to find a bakery around here that makes them like that, but nobody gets it right. They're always too dense or they use too much nutmeg. Nutmeg is such an overpowering spice, you really have to be careful with it. I put too much in a pumpkin pie I tried to bake for Thanksgiving two years ago, and it was practically inedible. My sister still teases me about it. She's actually coming to visit next month, and I need to figure out where we're going to eat. She's a vegetarian now, which makes picking a restaurant so complicated. \n\nBut yeah, no, it wasn't a sudden thing. It just slowly started happening.\n\nDoctor: Have you had at least two weeks when you were hearing the voice or felt people were tracking you, while your mood felt completely normal?\n\nPatient: Yeah, definitely. Like I was saying before, there was that calmer stretch where I was actually sleeping, like, seven or eight hours a night. I didn't have that feeling that I was running the whole market or anything, my mood was just... normal, I guess. But even then, for a few weeks, I still totally knew they were tracking my buys and messing with the numbers. And I was still hearing that guy's voice in the stockroom. \n\nIt's so annoying when I'm trying to work. Speaking of work, my manager, Dave, he just got this new car. It's one of those electric ones, a Tesla, I think? It's completely silent when it drives. He almost ran into me in the parking lot the other day because I didn't even hear him coming. I don't know how I feel about those electric cars. I mean, I get that they're good for the environment and all, but I feel like part of the experience of driving is hearing the engine, you know? My first car was this beat-up old Honda Civic, and the muffler was rusted out, so it sounded like a jet engine taking off every time I hit the gas. I loved that car. I drove it all the way to Florida once for spring break with some buddies. We stayed in this cheap motel that smelled like bleach and old carpet, but we had the best time. We practically lived on the beach. I got the worst sunburn of my life, though. I had to sleep on my stomach for three days. \n\nBut yeah, to answer your question, even when my mood was totally normal and I was sleeping fine, the tracking and the voice were still there for a few weeks.\n\n[Last Question]\nHave you ever had a period of at least one week when you felt unusually energetic or wired most of the day, nearly every day?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Male / 22 / wholesale_or_retail_buyer / high_school\n- severity: Moderate\n\n[Targeted Info]\nNone\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Tangent patient.\nA patient who goes off on tangents may\n1) begin answering the doctor's question but quickly shift to an unrelated topic,\n2) frequently add irrelevant personal anecdotes or experiences while answering the question, and\n3) have difficulty remaining focused on the doctor's question, potentially requiring the doctor to repeat the question or redirect the conversation to obtain clear clinical information.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Um, energetic? I mean... I don't know if I've had a whole week like that. I guess I haven't really noticed being wired all day. I drink a lot of coffee, though. We have this new espresso machine in the breakroom at work, and it's incredible. It grinds the beans right there, so it always smells amazing when you walk in. I used to be a barista back in high school, actually. It was my first job. I worked at this little independent shop downtown, not one of the big chains. We had to wear these awful green aprons, and I always smelled like roasted beans and stale milk by the end of my shift. 

My boss there was this guy named Marcus, and he was obsessed with jazz music. He'd play it all day long, just these really obscure trumpet solos. I actually tried to learn how to play the trumpet in middle school band, but I was terrible at it. I could never get my lips to buzz right in the mouthpiece. My dog at the time, Buster, he was a golden retriever mix, he would start howling every time I tried to practice. He was such a good dog. We found him at a shelter when I was ten, and he already knew how to shake paws. 

But yeah, I don't think I've had a week where I felt unusually wired or anything. Just the normal coffee energy, I guess.

Well, I know I just said I didn't think I had a whole week of being wired, but... I mean, when I get like that, when I'm really keyed up, yeah, I do a ton of stuff. I can't just handle one task at a time. I'll be emailing three different vendors at once, trying to put together a whole new buy plan for the quarter, and then I'm reorganizing all the fabric samples in the back room because they just look wrong. And I'll be pacing around the office the whole time, like if I stop moving, I'll completely lose the thread of what I'm doing. 

It's kind of like how my grandma used to knit. She could never just sit and watch TV; she always had to have her knitting needles going. She made me this massive, itchy wool sweater for Christmas when I was twelve, and it was bright orange. I hated it so much, but she was so proud of it. I had to wear it every time we went over to her house for Sunday dinner. She always made pot roast with these tiny little potatoes that soaked up all the gravy. I tried making a pot roast in my slow cooker last month, but I didn't sear the meat first, so it just tasted boiled. I watched this whole YouTube tutorial on how to do it right, but the guy in the video had this really annoying voice, so I kept skipping ahead and I must have missed the most important part. 

But yeah, when I'm feeling that energy, I'm definitely doing a lot more projects all at once. I just can't sit still.

My grandpa used to smoke those when we'd go fishing up at the lake. We caught this massive bass once, I swear it was like ten pounds. We had it mounted and it hung in his garage for years until the garage roof leaked and ruined it. It's a shame, really. But yeah, even when I'm sleeping fine and feeling normal, I still know they're tracking me and I still hear that voice.\n\nDoctor: When you hear that voice in the stockroom, does it sound like an actual voice speaking out loud?\n\nPatient: Yeah, it's definitely an actual voice. It's out loud. Sometimes when I'm in the stockroom, or even when I'm just driving back from a vendor meeting, I'll hear this man's voice. It sounds like it's coming from right behind me, just saying, \"Move faster, Scott,\" or he'll just start laughing at the items I picked for the order. And every time, I spin around to look, and there's absolutely nobody there. \n\nIt's really distracting when I'm driving, especially on the interstate. The traffic on I-95 has been completely insane lately because of that construction near the bridge. They've had the right lane closed for, like, six months now. I don't even know what they're fixing. I saw this huge crane there the other day, one of those massive yellow ones with the giant treads instead of wheels. I used to have a toy crane just like that when I was a kid. My dad bought it for me at this little hobby shop that used to be next to the bowling alley. We used to go bowling every Thursday night, and they had the best french fries. They were crinkle-cut and always super salty, and they served them in those little red plastic baskets with the wax paper liners. I tried to make crinkle-cut fries at home once, but I bought the wrong kind of potatoes. I used russets, but I think you're supposed to use Yukon Golds or something if you want them really crispy. \n\nBut yeah, the voice is out loud. It's right there behind me, telling me to move faster or laughing at me.\n\nDoctor: Did the voice and the belief that people at work were tracking your buys start very suddenly, within a couple of weeks?\n\nPatient: Um, no, I wouldn't say it was sudden. It just kind of... I don't know, it crept up on me, I guess. It wasn't like one day it was fine and the next I was hearing him. It was more gradual. Kind of like how the seasons change, you know? Like, you don't realize it's autumn until you step outside and suddenly the air just has that crisp smell to it. \n\nI love that smell, actually. It always reminds me of this apple orchard my mom used to take me to when I was little. We'd go every October, and they had these incredible cider donuts. They were always served warm, right out of the fryer, and just coated in cinnamon sugar. I've been trying to find a bakery around here that makes them like that, but nobody gets it right. They're always too dense or they use too much nutmeg. Nutmeg is such an overpowering spice, you really have to be careful with it. I put too much in a pumpkin pie I tried to bake for Thanksgiving two years ago, and it was practically inedible. My sister still teases me about it. She's actually coming to visit next month, and I need to figure out where we're going to eat. She's a vegetarian now, which makes picking a restaurant so complicated. \n\nBut yeah, no, it wasn't a sudden thing. It just slowly started happening.\n\nDoctor: Have you had at least two weeks when you were hearing the voice or felt people were tracking you, while your mood felt completely normal?\n\nPatient: Yeah, definitely. Like I was saying before, there was that calmer stretch where I was actually sleeping, like, seven or eight hours a night. I didn't have that feeling that I was running the whole market or anything, my mood was just... normal, I guess. But even then, for a few weeks, I still totally knew they were tracking my buys and messing with the numbers. And I was still hearing that guy's voice in the stockroom. \n\nIt's so annoying when I'm trying to work. Speaking of work, my manager, Dave, he just got this new car. It's one of those electric ones, a Tesla, I think? It's completely silent when it drives. He almost ran into me in the parking lot the other day because I didn't even hear him coming. I don't know how I feel about those electric cars. I mean, I get that they're good for the environment and all, but I feel like part of the experience of driving is hearing the engine, you know? My first car was this beat-up old Honda Civic, and the muffler was rusted out, so it sounded like a jet engine taking off every time I hit the gas. I loved that car. I drove it all the way to Florida once for spring break with some buddies. We stayed in this cheap motel that smelled like bleach and old carpet, but we had the best time. We practically lived on the beach. I got the worst sunburn of my life, though. I had to sleep on my stomach for three days. \n\nBut yeah, to answer your question, even when my mood was totally normal and I was sleeping fine, the tracking and the voice were still there for a few weeks.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a period of at least one week when you felt unusually energetic or wired most of the day, nearly every day?\n\nPatient: Um, energetic? I mean... I don't know if I've had a whole week like that. I guess I haven't really noticed being wired all day. I drink a lot of coffee, though. We found him at a shelter when I was ten, and he already knew how to shake paws. \n\nBut yeah, I don't think I've had a week where I felt unusually wired or anything. Just the normal coffee energy, I guess.\n\nDoctor: During those times when you felt unusually energetic, were you doing a lot more activities or projects than usual?\n\nPatient: Well, I know I just said I didn't think I had a whole week of being wired, but...
